where the explicit form of the matrix elements is given by the Boltzmann weights (
5.1
). The permutation matrix
P
acts in
\mathbb{C}^{n+m+1}\otimes\mathbb{C}^{n+m+1}
. The supersymmetric
R
-matrix is defined as before:

(5.8)

\displaystyle R(x)=P\check{R}(x)

Let
\id
be the identity matrix in
\mathbb{C}^{n+m+1}
then
\check{R}
,
R
and
P
act in
\otimes_{i=1}^{N}V_{x_{i}}
by
\check{R}_{i}(x_{i+1}/x_{i})
,
R_{i}(x_{i+1}/x_{i})
and
P_{i}
respectively (see e.g. (
4.13
)). For these matrices we have the Yang–Baxter equation (
4.14
) and the same unitarity relation as before (
4.15
). We will also use the same graphical representation for
\check{R}_{i}(x_{i+1}/x_{i})
as before.

The tensors
Z_{N}(x;y),W_{N}(x;y)
and
W_{N}(x)
in the
sl_{n+1|m}
case are defined algebraically by (
4.17
), (
4.18
) and (
4.19
) respectively and their matrix elements are

Z_{\alpha,\gamma}^{\beta,\delta}(x;y),\quad W_{\gamma}^{\delta}(x;y)\quad\text{and}~~~W_{\gamma}^{\delta}(x)

with the indices
\alpha,\beta,\gamma,\delta\in\{0,1\ldots n+m\}^{N}
. The graphical interpretation of these matrix elements is given by Definition
5.1
and appropriate specialization of the indices and parameters in the case of
W_{\gamma}^{\delta}(x;y)
and
W_{\gamma}^{\delta}(x)
. We have the analogue of Lemma
4.3
.

Lemma 5.2
.

For
i=1\ldots N-1
,
the tensor
Z_{N}(x;y)
satisfies the following exchange relations:

(5.9)

\displaystyle\check{R}_{i}(x_{i+1}/x_{i})Z_{N}(x;y)=Z_{N}(\ldots x_{i+1},x_{i}\ldots;y)\check{R}_{N+i}(x_{i+1}/x_{i})

(5.10)

\displaystyle Z_{N}(x;y)\check{R}_{i}(y_{i}/y_{i+1})=\check{R}_{N+i}(y_{i}/y_{i+1})Z_{N}(x;\ldots y_{i+1},y_{i}\ldots)

The tensor
W_{N}(x;y)
satisfies:

(5.11)

\displaystyle W_{N}(x;y)

\displaystyle=W_{N}(\ldots x_{i+1},x_{i}\ldots;y)\check{R}_{i}(x_{i+1}/x_{i})

(5.12)

\displaystyle W_{N}(x;y)

\displaystyle=\check{R}_{i}(y_{i}/y_{i+1})W_{N}(x;\ldots y_{i+1},y_{i}\ldots)

and for
W_{N}(x)
we have:

(5.13)

\displaystyle\check{R}_{i}(x_{i+1}/x_{i})W_{N}(x)=W_{N}(\ldots x_{i+1},x_{i}\ldots)\check{R}_{i}(x_{i+1}/x_{i})

This Lemma is based on the same algebraic identities as Lemma
4.3
.

5.2.
The
sl_{n+1|m}

F
-matrix and transformed tensors

In this section we introduce the supersymmetric
sl_{n+1|m}

F
-matrix
[
44
]
. This matrix satisfies the same properties as the six vertex
F
-matrix therefore we will immediately get the transformed tensors
\widetilde{Z}_{N}(x;y),\widetilde{W}_{N}(x;y)
and
\widetilde{W}_{N}(x)
with the desired properties establishes in Section
4.2
.

Definition 5.3
.

For
k,l\in\{0\ldots n+m\}
and
r=1\dots N
, let
E^{(kl)}_{r}\in{\text{End}}\left(\otimes_{i=1}^{N}V_{x_{i}}\right)
be the matrix acting non-trivially on the
r
-th tensor space
V_{x_{r}}
by the
(n+m+1)\times(n+m+1)
matrix unit
E^{(kl)}
. The
N
-site supersymmetric
sl_{n+1|m}

F
-matrix reads:

(5.14)

\displaystyle F_{N}(x_{1}\dots x_{N})=\sum_{\sigma\in\mathcal{S}_{N}}\sum_{(k_{1}\dots k_{N})\in\mathcal{J}_{\sigma}}\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq i<j\leq N\\
k_{i}=k_{j}>n\end{subarray}}\frac{x_{\sigma(i)}+x_{\sigma(j)}}{x_{\sigma(i)}-tx_{\sigma(j)}}\cdot\prod_{i=1}^{N}E^{(k_{i}k_{i})}_{\sigma(i)}R_{\sigma}

where
R_{\sigma}
is defined as before (
4.34
) but based on the
sl_{n+1|m}

R
-matrix and the set
\mathcal{J}_{\sigma}
is defined by:

(5.15)

\displaystyle\mathcal{J}_{\sigma}=\Big\{0\leq k_{1}\leq\dots\leq k_{N}\leq n+m:k_{i}<k_{i+1}~~~\text{if}~~~\sigma(i)>\sigma(i+1)\Big\}

The
F
-matrix (
5.14
) satisfies the property:

(5.16)

\displaystyle F_{N}(x_{1}\dots x_{N})=P_{\sigma}F_{N}(x_{\sigma(1)}\ldots x_{\sigma(N)})\check{R}_{\sigma}

We note that the
F
-matrix in the supersymmetric case is modified compared to (
4.37
) by the additional rational function in (
5.14
). Each factor of this rational function is associated to a pair of fermionic labels, therefore if
m=0
, this factor disappears and one recovers the standard
sl_{n+1}

F
-matrix (see Appendix
B
for further discussion of this
F
-matrix).

Lemma 5.4
.

Fix a composition of non-negative integers
(l_{0},l_{1}\ldots l_{n+m})
such that
N=l_{0}+\cdots+l_{n+m}
. For every such composition we define two ordered compositions
\lambda^{-}
and
\lambda^{+}
:

(5.17)

\displaystyle\lambda^{-}:=(0^{l_{0}}1^{l_{1}}\ldots(n+m)^{l_{n+m}}),\qquad\lambda^{+}:=((n+m)^{l_{n+m}}\ldots 1^{l_{1}}0^{l_{0}})

The supersymmetric
F
-matrix has the properties:

(5.18)

\displaystyle\left\langle\lambda^{-}\right|F_{N}(x)=\left\langle\lambda^{-}\right|\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq i<j\leq N\\
\lambda^{-}_{i}=\lambda^{-}_{j}>n\end{subarray}}\frac{x_{i}+x_{j}}{x_{i}-tx_{j}},\qquad F_{N}(x)\left|\lambda^{+}\right\rangle=\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq i<j\leq N\\
\lambda^{+}_{i}\neq\lambda^{+}_{j}\end{subarray}}t^{\delta_{\lambda_{j}>0}}\dfrac{x_{i}-x_{j}}{x_{i}-tx_{j}}\cdot\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq i<j\leq N\\
\lambda^{+}_{i}=\lambda^{+}_{j}>n\end{subarray}}\frac{x_{i}+x_{j}}{x_{i}-tx_{j}}\left|\lambda^{+}\right\rangle

The proof of this lemma is presented in Appendix
B.2

Definition 5.5
.

Let
F_{N}
be the supersymmetric
F
-matrix (
5.14
). Introduce transformed tensors
\widetilde{Z}_{N}(x;y)
,
\widetilde{W}_{N}(x;y)
and
\widetilde{W}_{N}(x)
:

(5.19)

\displaystyle\widetilde{Z}_{N}(x;y)

\displaystyle:=\left(F_{N}(x)\otimes F_{N}(y)\right)Z_{N}(x;y)\left(F^{-1}_{N}(y)\otimes F^{-1}_{N}(x)\right)

(5.20)

\displaystyle\widetilde{W}_{N}(x;y)

\displaystyle:=F_{N}(y)W_{N}(x;y)F^{-1}_{N}(x)

(5.21)

\displaystyle\widetilde{W}_{N}(x)

\displaystyle:=F_{N}(x)W_{N}(x)F^{-1}_{N}(x)

Proposition 5.6
.

For
i=1\ldots N-1
,
the tensor
\widetilde{Z}_{N}(x;y)
satisfies the following exchange relations:

(5.22)

\displaystyle P_{i}\widetilde{Z}(x;y)

\displaystyle=\widetilde{Z}(\ldots x_{i+1},x_{i}\ldots;y)P_{N+i}

(5.23)

\displaystyle\widetilde{Z}(x;y)P_{i}

\displaystyle=P_{N+i}\widetilde{Z}(x;\ldots y_{i+1},y_{i}\ldots)

the tensor
\widetilde{W}_{N}(x;y)
satisfies:

(5.24)

\displaystyle\widetilde{W}_{N}(x;y)P_{i}

\displaystyle=\widetilde{W}_{N}(\ldots x_{i+1},x_{i}\ldots;y)

(5.25)

\displaystyle P_{i}\widetilde{W}_{N}(x;y)

\displaystyle=\widetilde{W}_{N}(x;\ldots y_{i+1},y_{i}\ldots)

and
\widetilde{W}_{N}(x)
satisfies:

(5.26)

\displaystyle P_{i}\widetilde{W}_{N}(x)

\displaystyle=\widetilde{W}_{N}(\ldots x_{i+1},x_{i}\ldots)P_{i}

The proof of this proposition is the same as the proof of Proposition
4.8
since the algebraic properties of the more general objects are the same as in the six vertex case. In the remaining part of this subsection we compute the matrix elements of
\widetilde{W}_{N}(x;y)
generalizing the result of Proposition
4.10
. The formula is again a product consisting of fully factorized terms and domain-wall partition functions:

(5.27)

\displaystyle D_{M}^{(k)}(x;y):=W_{(k^{M})}^{(k^{M})}(x_{1}\ldots x_{M};y_{1}\ldots y_{M}).

When the label
k
is fermionic (i.e.
k>n
) the domain-wall partition function becomes the fermionic version of the six vertex domain-wall partition function and is known to have a factorized formula
[
21
]
. We summarize the formulas for
D_{M}^{(k)}(x;y)
for different
k
in the following Lemma.

Lemma 5.7
.

D_{M}^{(k)}(x;y)
is the domain-wall partition function associated to the label
k
:

(5.28)

\displaystyle D_{M}^{(k)}(x;y)=\begin{cases}1&k=0\\
D_{M}(x;y)&0<k\leq n\\
(-1+t)^{M}\dfrac{\prod_{i=1}^{M}x_{i}\prod_{j=i+1}^{M}(x_{j}-tx_{i})(y_{i}-ty_{j})}{\prod_{i,j=1}^{M}(tx_{i}-y_{j})}&n<k\leq n+m\end{cases}

where
D_{M}(x;y)
is defined in (
4.52
).

Proposition 5.8
.

Fix a composition of non-negative integers
(l_{0},l_{1}\ldots l_{n+m})
such that
N=l_{0}+\cdots+l_{n+m}
. Let
\alpha,\beta
be two permutations of
(0^{l_{0}}1^{l_{1}}\ldots(n+m)^{l_{n+m}})
and
P(k),S(k)\subseteq[N]
be pairs of compositions which record the positions of
k
’s in
\alpha
and
\beta
respectively, then:

(5.29)

\displaystyle\widetilde{W}_{\alpha}^{\beta}(x;y)=\prod_{k=0}^{n+m}D_{l_{k}}^{(k)}(x_{S(k)},y_{P(k)})

\displaystyle\times\prod_{0\leq k_{1}<k_{2}\leq n+m}\prod_{j\in S(k_{1})}\prod_{i\in S(k_{2})}\frac{x_{i}-tx_{j}}{x_{i}-x_{j}}\cdot\prod_{j\in S(k_{1})}\prod_{i\in P(k_{2})}\frac{y_{i}-x_{j}}{y_{i}-tx_{j}}

\displaystyle\times\prod_{k=n+1}^{n+m}\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}i,j\in P(k)\\
i<j\end{subarray}}\frac{y_{i}+y_{j}}{y_{i}-ty_{j}}\cdot\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}i,j\in S(k)\\
i<j\end{subarray}}\frac{x_{i}-tx_{j}}{x_{i}+x_{j}}

Proof.

The proof is analogous to the proof of Proposition
4.10
. Define two compositions
\lambda^{\pm}
:

\displaystyle\lambda^{-}:=(0^{l_{0}}1^{l_{1}}\ldots(n+m)^{l_{n+m}}),\qquad\lambda^{+}:=((n+m)^{l_{n+m}}\ldots 1^{l_{1}}0^{l_{0}})

For two permutations
\sigma,\tau\in\mathcal{S}_{N}
let
\alpha=\sigma^{-1}(\lambda^{-})
and
\beta=\tau^{-1}(\lambda^{-})
. Represent the vectors
\left\langle\alpha\right|
and
\left|\beta\right\rangle
as follows:

\left\langle\alpha\right|=\left\langle\lambda^{-}\right|P_{\sigma^{-1}},\qquad\left|\beta\right\rangle=P_{\tau}\left|\lambda^{-}\right\rangle

Different matrix elements
\widetilde{W}_{\alpha}^{\beta}(x;y)
are related to each other by appropriately permuting the spectral parameters:

(5.30)

\displaystyle\widetilde{W}_{\alpha}^{\beta}(x;y)=\left\langle\alpha\right|\widetilde{W}_{N}(x;y)\left|\beta\right\rangle=\left\langle\lambda^{-}\right|P_{\sigma^{-1}}\widetilde{W}_{N}(x;y)P_{\tau}\left|\lambda^{-}\right\rangle=\left\langle\lambda^{-}\right|\widetilde{W}_{N}(x_{\tau};y_{\sigma})\left|\lambda^{-}\right\rangle=\widetilde{W}_{\lambda^{-}}^{\lambda^{-}}(x_{\tau};y_{\sigma})

where we used the properties (
5.24
) and (
5.25
) of
\widetilde{W}_{\alpha}^{\beta}(x;y)
. Consider next the matrix element
\widetilde{W}_{\lambda^{-}}^{\lambda^{+}}(x;y)
and write it in terms of
W_{\lambda^{-}}^{\lambda^{+}}(x;y)
using (
5.20
):

\displaystyle\widetilde{W}_{\lambda^{-}}^{\lambda^{+}}(x;y)

\displaystyle=\left\langle\lambda^{-}\right|F_{N}(y)W_{N}(x;y)F^{-1}_{N}(x)\left|\lambda^{+}\right\rangle

(5.31)

\displaystyle=\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq i<j\leq N\\
\lambda^{+}_{i}\neq\lambda^{+}_{j}\end{subarray}}t^{-\delta_{\lambda^{+}_{j}>0}}\dfrac{x_{i}-tx_{j}}{x_{i}-x_{j}}\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq i<j\leq N\\
\lambda^{+}_{i}=\lambda^{+}_{j}>n\end{subarray}}\frac{x_{i}-tx_{j}}{x_{i}+x_{j}}\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq i<j\leq N\\
\lambda^{-}_{i}=\lambda^{-}_{j}>n\end{subarray}}\frac{y_{i}+y_{j}}{y_{i}-ty_{j}}\times W_{\lambda^{-}}^{\lambda^{+}}(x;y)

where in the second line we used the properties (
5.18
) of the supersymmetric
F
-matrix. The matrix element
W_{\lambda^{-}}^{\lambda^{+}}(x;y)
can be evaluated in a similar way as the matrix element
W_{(0^{N-k}1^{k})}^{(1^{k}0^{N-k})}(x;y)
in the proof of Proposition
4.10
. For demonstration we choose
N=6
,
m=n=1
and
l_{0}=l_{1}=l_{2}=2
. The graphical depiction of the partition function
W_{(001122)}^{(221100)}(x;y)
shows several regions which are frozen and two regions which contain domain-wall type configurations which we enclosed in the dotted squares for the reference:

(5.32)

\displaystyle=\prod_{i\in\{3,4,5,6\}}\prod_{j\in\{5,6\}}\frac{y_{i}-x_{j}}{y_{i}-tx_{j}}\prod_{i\in\{5,6\}}\prod_{j\in\{3,4\}}t\frac{y_{i}-x_{j}}{y_{i}-tx_{j}}\times D_{2}^{(1)}(x_{3},x_{4};y_{3},y_{4})D_{2}^{(2)}(x_{1},x_{2};y_{5},y_{6})

The partial freezing of the last two columns in the graphical representation given in the first line in (
5.32
) is due to the fact that the
2
’s on the right boundary must take either left or down steps and drop to the bottom boundary in the last two columns. This leaves us with domain-wall configurations in the intersections of the first two rows and last two columns. The remaining part of the lattice is of the form (
4.55
). As a result we need to evaluate two domain-wall partition functions
D_{2}^{(1)}
and
D_{2}^{(2)}
as indicated in the second line in (
5.32
) and take into account various factors coming from the vertices:

\scriptstyle 0
\scriptstyle 0
\scriptstyle 1
\scriptstyle 1

\scriptstyle 0
\scriptstyle 0
\scriptstyle 2
\scriptstyle 2

\scriptstyle 1
\scriptstyle 1
\scriptstyle 2
\scriptstyle 2

where the first two are of the fourth type in (
5.1
) and the third one is of the third type in (
5.1
) (recall that
0
is considered the highest colour label).

Let us view the lattice configurations in the first line in (
5.32
) as a block matrix.
The example of (
5.32
) shows that the anti-diagonal blocks must have domain-wall configurations, above the anti-diagonal the configurations are trivial with all edges labelled
0
and below the anti-diagonal we have blocks with vertices of type four and type three in (
5.1
). The latter blocks can be expressed in terms of
Z_{\alpha,\beta}^{\beta,\alpha}
with
\alpha=(i^{M})
and
\beta=(j^{K})
for some
i<j
and evaluated:

(5.33)

\displaystyle Z_{(i^{M}),(j^{K})}^{(j^{K}),(i^{M})}(x;y)=t^{\delta_{i>0}MK}\prod_{a=1}^{M}\prod_{b=1}^{K}\frac{y_{b}-x_{a}}{y_{b}-tx_{a}}

We can write
W^{\lambda^{+}}_{\lambda^{-}}(x;y)
in terms of such block matrix:

\displaystyle W^{\lambda^{+}}_{\lambda^{-}}(x;y)=\prod_{a,b}\left(\begin{array}[]{ccccc}1&\ldots&1&\ldots&W_{m+n}\\
\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\iddots&\vdots\\
1&\ldots&W_{k}&\ldots&W_{k,m+n}\\
\vdots&\iddots&\vdots&\vdots&\vdots\\
W_{0}&\ldots&W_{0,k}&\ldots&W_{0,m+n}\\
\end{array}\right)_{a,b}

where we shortened the notations:

(5.39)

\displaystyle W_{k}=D_{l_{k}}^{(k)}(x^{(k)},y^{(k)}),\qquad W_{i,j}=Z_{(i^{l_{i}}),(j^{l_{j}})}^{(j^{l_{j}}),(i^{l_{i}})}(x^{(i)},y^{(j)})

and
(x^{(k)})
and
(y^{(k)})
denote
l_{k}
spectral parameters
x_{a}
and
y_{b}
with
a
and
b
such that
\lambda^{+}_{a}=k
and
\lambda^{-}_{b}=k
for all
a,b
. By evaluating the product in (
5.2
) we get:

(5.40)

\displaystyle W_{\lambda^{-}}^{\lambda^{+}}(x;y)=\prod_{k=0}^{n+m}D_{l_{k}}^{(k)}(x^{(k)},y^{(k)})\prod_{0\leq i<j\leq n+m}Z_{(i^{l_{i}}),(j^{l_{j}})}^{(j^{l_{j}}),(i^{l_{i}})}(x^{(i)},y^{(j)})

This expression allows us to compute
\widetilde{W}_{\lambda^{-}}^{\lambda^{+}}(x;y)
using (
5.31
). The powers of
t
appearing in (
5.31
) combine into
t^{-l_{0}(N-l_{0})}
and, after the substitution of (
5.40
) into (
5.31
), are canceled against the powers of
t
coming from (
5.33
). After inserting
\widetilde{W}_{\lambda^{-}}^{\lambda^{+}}(x;y)
into (
5.30
) we get the final expression (
5.29
) for all matrix elements
\widetilde{W}_{\alpha}^{\beta}(x;y)
.
∎

5.3.
Trace of
W_{N}(x)
and the shuffle product

In this section we define the supersymmetric partition function
T_{N}(x)
as the trace of the matrix
W_{N}(x)
. We show that the trace can be rewritten as a symmetrization over the parameters
x_{1}\ldots x_{N}
acting on specific diagonal elements of
\widetilde{W}_{N}(x)
. These diagonal elements can be computed using Proposition
5.8
. The result of this computation, summarized in Theorem
5.11
, gives a formula for
T_{N}(x)
in terms of the shuffle product of
\mathcal{A}^{\circ}
.

Definition 5.9
.

Let
z_{0},z_{1}\ldots z_{n}
and
w_{1}\ldots w_{m}
be two sets of indeterminates. Define the following function:

(5.41)

\displaystyle T_{N}(x;z,w):=\sum_{\alpha\in\{0,1\ldots n+m\}^{N}}\prod_{i=0}^{n}z_{\alpha_{i}}\prod_{i=1}^{m}(-w_{\alpha_{n+i}})\left\langle\alpha\right|W_{N}(x)\left|\alpha\right\rangle

The graphical counterpart of (
5.41
) is given by (
4.58
) with vertices interpreted as in (
5.1
).
Using this we can see that the function
T_{N}(x;z,w)
is equal to the conic partition function
Z_{N}(x;z,w)
which was defined in the introduction (
1.4
) using lattice paths. The matrix
W_{N}(x)
is block diagonal with blocks labelled by a collection of non-negative integers
(l_{0},l_{1}\ldots l_{n+m})
where
l_{k}
is the number of
k
’s in
\alpha
and
\beta
in the matrix elements
W_{\alpha}^{\beta}(x)
. This means that
T_{N}(x;z,w)
is the (graded) trace of
W_{N}(x)
where the monomials in
z
and
w
parameterize different blocks of
W_{N}(x)
.

Lemma 5.10
.

The function
T_{N}(x;z,w)
is symmetric in
(x_{1}\ldots x_{N})
.

The proof follows the same lines as the proof of Lemma
4.12
. We arrive at the main theorem of this section.

Theorem 5.11
.

The function
T_{N}(x;z,w)
can be written as a shuffle product:

\displaystyle T_{N}(x;z,w)=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}l_{0}\ldots l_{n+m}\geq 0\\
l_{0}+\cdots+l_{n+m}=N\end{subarray}}

\displaystyle\prod_{i=0}^{n}z_{i}^{l_{i}}\prod_{i=1}^{m}(-w_{i})^{l_{n+i}}\frac{(1-t^{-1})^{N-l_{0}}}{(1-qt^{-1})^{N-l_{0}}}

(5.42)

\displaystyle E_{l_{n+m}}(t^{-1})*\dots*E_{l_{n+1}}(t^{-1})*H_{l_{n}}(t^{-1})*\cdots*H_{l_{1}}(t^{-1})*E_{l_{0}}(tq^{-1})

Proof.

By the same arguments leading to (
4.61
) we convert the trace in (
5.41
) into a summation over the symmetric group:

(5.43)

\displaystyle T_{N}(x;z,w)=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}l_{0}\ldots l_{n+m}\geq 0\\
l_{0}+\cdots+l_{n+m}=N\end{subarray}}\frac{1}{l_{0}!\cdots l_{n+m}!}\prod_{i=0}^{n}z_{i}^{l_{i}}\prod_{i=1}^{m}(-w_{i})^{l_{n+i}}\sum_{\sigma\in\mathcal{S}_{N}}\widetilde{W}_{\sigma(\lambda^{-})}^{\sigma(\lambda^{-})}(x)

where
\lambda^{-}=(0^{l_{0}}1^{l_{1}}\ldots(n+m)^{l_{n+m}})
.
Let us show that the summation over
\sigma
can be written as a shuffle product. The diagonal elements
\widetilde{W}_{\alpha}^{\alpha}(x)
in (
5.43
) can be computed with the help of Proposition
5.8
in which we set
y_{i}=qx_{i}
6
6

6

We note that in the expression (
5.44
) the eigenvalues of the
F
-matrix from (
5.18
) appear in the form of a ratio. This ratio is the only explicit information about the
F
-matrix which is needed to compute
T_{N}
.
:

(5.44)

\displaystyle\widetilde{W}_{\alpha}^{\alpha}(x)=\prod_{k=0}^{n+m}D_{l_{k}}^{(k)}(x_{S(k)})\times\prod_{k_{1}=0}^{n+m}\prod_{k_{2}=k_{1}+1}^{n+m}\prod_{j\in S(k_{1})}\prod_{i\in S(k_{2})}\frac{x_{i}-tx_{j}}{x_{i}-x_{j}}\frac{qx_{i}-x_{j}}{qx_{i}-tx_{j}}

where
D_{M}^{(k)}(x):=D_{M}^{(k)}(x_{1}\ldots x_{M};qx_{1}\ldots qx_{M})
and
S(k)\in[N]
is the subset that records the positions of
k
’s in
\alpha
. The factors
D_{l_{k}}^{(k)}(x)
are the domain-wall partition functions from Lemma
5.7
in the special case
y_{i}=qx_{i}
. We can match these partition functions with the shuffle algebra functions:

(5.45)

\displaystyle D_{M}^{(k)}(x)=\begin{cases}E_{M}(tq^{-1})&k=0\\
\dfrac{(1-t^{-1})^{M}}{(1-qt^{-1})^{M}}H_{M}(t^{-1})&0<k\leq n\\
\dfrac{(1-t^{-1})^{M}}{(1-qt^{-1})^{M}}E_{M}(t^{-1})&n<k\leq n+m\end{cases}

where we remind that
E_{M}(tq^{-1})=1
but considered to be a function in
(x_{1}\ldots x_{M})
. By inserting (
5.45
) into (
5.44
) and recalling the definition of
\zeta
in (
3.2
) we get:

(5.46)

\displaystyle\widetilde{W}_{\alpha}^{\alpha}(x)=\frac{(1-t^{-1})^{N-l_{0}}}{(1-qt^{-1})^{N-l_{0}}}E_{l_{0}}(tq^{-1};x_{S(0)})

\displaystyle\prod_{k=1}^{n}H_{l_{k}}(t^{-1};x_{S(k)})\prod_{k=n+1}^{n+m}E_{l_{k}}(t^{-1};x_{S(k)})

\displaystyle\prod_{k_{1}=0}^{n+m}\prod_{k_{2}=k_{1}+1}^{n+m}\prod_{i\in S(k_{1})}\prod_{j\in S(k_{2})}\zeta(x_{j}/x_{i})

Inserting this formula for
\widetilde{W}_{\alpha}^{\alpha}(x)
into (
5.43
) gives an expression which can be rewritten using the shuffle product (
3.4
). Note that the positions of the variables
x_{j}
and
x_{i}
in
\zeta(x_{j}/x_{i})
decide the order in which the shuffle product is taken: the functions (
H
or
E
) which depend on
S(k)
with higher values of
k
should be placed on the left. This explains the reason for the ordering of the factors in the shuffle product in (
5.42
).
∎

Corollary 5.12
.

Consider the generating function of
T_{N}(x;z,w)
:

(5.47)

\displaystyle T(v;z,w)=\sum_{N=0}^{\infty}v^{N}T_{N}(x;z,w)

We have:

(5.48)

\displaystyle T(v;z,w)=\exp_{*}\left(\sum_{k>0}\frac{1}{k}\frac{1-t^{k}}{1-q^{k}}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{m}w_{i}^{k}-\sum_{i=1}^{n}z_{i}^{k}-\frac{q^{k}-t^{k}}{1-t^{k}}z_{0}^{k}\right)v^{k}S_{k}\right)

Proof.

We first note that the elements
E_{k}(t^{-1})
,
H_{k}(t^{-1})
and
E_{k}(tq^{-1})
belong to the commutative shuffle algebra
\mathcal{A}^{\circ}
. The proof of (
5.48
) follows by writing (
5.47
) with
T_{N}
given by (
5.42
). Then summing over
N
produces a shuffle product of
m
generating functions of
E_{k}(t^{-1})
,
n
generating functions of
H_{k}(t^{-1})
and a single generating function of
E_{k}(tq^{-1})
where all generating functions have different generating parameters. Then we can use the exponential generating functions (
3.16
) and (
3.17
) which allows us to rewrite
T(v;z,w)
in the form given in (
5.48
). The last step is possible because we are dealing with commutative elements.
∎

The partition function
T_{N}(v;z,w)
is equal to
Z_{N}
from the introduction (
1.4
). In Theorem
1.1
we wrote
Z_{N}
in terms of yet another conic partition function
L_{N}
. This result follows from (
5.51
) below.

Definition 5.13
.

Consider the matrix
W_{N}(x)
associated to
U_{t}(\widehat{sl}_{2|1})
vertex model, i.e. in the matrix elements
W_{\gamma}^{\delta}(x)
, with
\gamma,\delta\in\{0,1,2\}^{N}
, the label
1
denotes the bosonic colour and
2
denotes the fermionic colour.
We define
L_{N}(x)
to be the following partition function:

(5.49)

\displaystyle L_{N}(x):=\sum_{\alpha\in\{1,2\}^{N}}(-1)^{m_{2}(\alpha)}m_{2}(\alpha)\left\langle\alpha\right|W_{N}(x)\left|\alpha\right\rangle

where
m_{2}(\alpha)
is the multiplicity of the fermionic index
2
in
\alpha
.

Using the same methods as in the proof of Theorem
5.11
we can write
L_{N}(x)
in terms of a shuffle product of
H_{M}(t^{-1})
and
E_{M}(t^{-1})
which by (
5.45
) are equal to
D^{(1)}_{M}
and
D^{(2)}_{M}
up to a factor. Therefore the partition function
L_{N}(x)
can be expressed as a combination of the fermionic and bosonic six vertex domain-wall partition functions:

(5.50)

\displaystyle L_{N}(x)=\sum_{j=1}^{N}(-1)^{j}jD_{N-j}^{(1)}*D_{j}^{(2)}

The following proposition provides a lattice path realization of the shuffle algebra elements
S_{N}\in\mathcal{A}^{\circ}_{N}
.

Proposition 5.14
.

The partition functions
L_{N}(x)
and the elements
S_{N}\in\mathcal{A}^{\circ}_{N}
are equal up to a factor:

(5.51)

\displaystyle L_{N}(x)=\frac{1-t^{N}}{1-q^{N}}S_{N}(x)

Proof.

Consider
T_{N}(x;z_{0},z_{1},w_{1})
, i.e. the case
n=m=1
of
T_{N}
.
Set
z_{0}=0,z_{1}=1
and
w_{1}=w
. We compute
T_{N}(x;0,1,w)
using the definition (
5.41
) of
T_{N}
:

(5.52)

\displaystyle T_{N}(x;0,1,w)=\sum_{\alpha\in\{1,2\}^{N}}(-w)^{m_{2}(\alpha)}\left\langle\alpha\right|W_{N}(x)\left|\alpha\right\rangle

By comparing (
5.49
) with (
5.52
) we see that the functions
L_{N}(x)
and
T_{N}(x;0,1,w)
are related by:

(5.53)

\displaystyle L_{N}(x)=\lim_{w\rightarrow 1}\frac{\partial}{\partial w}T_{N}(x;0,1,w)

Using (
5.48
) we compute the exponential generating function of
T_{N}(x;0,1,w)
:

(5.54)

\displaystyle\sum_{N=0}^{\infty}v^{N}T_{N}(x;0,1,w)=\exp_{*}\left(\sum_{k>0}\frac{1}{k}\frac{1-t^{k}}{1-q^{k}}\left(w^{k}-1\right)v^{k}S_{k}\right)

Next we take the sum over
N
on both sides in (
5.53
) with
v^{N}
and compute the derivative and the limit on the r.h.s. using (
5.54
). As a result we get (
5.51
).
∎

6.
Skew Macdonald functions and lattice paths

In this section we derive the connection between the trace elements
T_{N}
and the skew Macdonald functions
P_{\mu/\nu}
. This connection is based on the mixed Cauchy kernel
K(x;y)
, which is defined by applying the isomorphism
\iota_{z}
to the Cauchy kernel
\Pi(z,y)
(see
[
15
]
). Under the evaluation map
\text{ev}_{\mu/\nu}
the mixed Cauchy kernel
K(x;y)
reproduces the skew Macdonald functions
P_{\mu/\nu}(y)
. Finally we relate the mixed Cauchy kernel with the generating function
T(v;z,w)
.

6.1.
The mixed Cauchy kernel

Let us recall the commuting shuffle algebra elements
F_{\lambda}\in\mathcal{A}^{\circ}
and the evaluation map (
3.3
) from Section
3
. The functions
F_{\lambda}=F_{\lambda}(x_{1}\ldots x_{k})
, with
\lambda\vdash k
, have an important property:

\text{ev}_{\mu}(F_{\lambda})=\delta_{\lambda,\mu}\frac{1}{d_{\lambda}}

Let us compute the evaluations
\text{ev}_{\mu/\nu}
of
F_{\lambda}
.

Lemma 6.1
.

We have the product rule:

(6.1)

\displaystyle F_{\lambda}*F_{\nu}=\sum_{\mu}q^{n(\mu^{\prime})-n(\lambda^{\prime})-n(\nu^{\prime})}\frac{c_{\lambda}c_{\nu}}{c_{\mu}}f^{\mu}_{\lambda,\nu}F_{\mu}

and evaluations:

(6.2)

\displaystyle{\text{ev}}_{\mu/\nu}\left(F_{\lambda}\right)=q^{n(\mu^{\prime})-n(\lambda^{\prime})-n(\nu^{\prime})}\frac{c_{\lambda}c_{\nu}}{c_{\mu}d_{\mu/\nu}}f^{\mu}_{\lambda,\nu}

Proof.

We recall the product rule for Macdonald functions (
2.13
):

P_{\lambda}P_{\nu}=\sum_{\mu}f^{\mu}_{\lambda,\nu}P_{\mu}

and apply the isomorphism
\iota^{-1}
to the l.h.s. and to the r.h.s. of this equation separately:

\displaystyle\iota^{-1}\left(P_{\lambda}P_{\nu}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{q^{n(\lambda^{\prime})+n(\nu^{\prime})}(1-t)^{|\lambda|+|\nu|}}{c_{\lambda}c_{\nu}}F_{\lambda}*F_{\nu}

\displaystyle\sum_{\mu}f^{\mu}_{\lambda,\nu}\iota^{-1}\left(P_{\mu}\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\mu}\frac{q^{n(\mu^{\prime})}(1-t)^{|\mu|}}{c_{\mu}}f^{\mu}_{\lambda,\nu}F_{\mu}

By comparing the two equations above and noting that
|\lambda|+|\mu|=|\nu|
by the degree argument we verify (
6.1
). In order to prove (
6.2
) we act with (
6.1
) on
\left|\varnothing\right\rangle
and recall (
3.36
) that
F_{\nu}\left|\varnothing\right\rangle=\left|\nu\right\rangle
:

\displaystyle F_{\lambda}\left|\nu\right\rangle=\sum_{\mu}q^{n(\mu^{\prime})-n(\lambda^{\prime})-n(\nu^{\prime})}\frac{c_{\lambda}c_{\nu}}{c_{\mu}}f^{\mu}_{\lambda,\nu}\left|\mu\right\rangle

This equation allows us to compute the matrix elements
\left\langle\mu\right|F_{\lambda}\left|\nu\right\rangle
:

\displaystyle\left\langle\mu\right|F_{\lambda}\left|\nu\right\rangle=q^{n(\mu^{\prime})-n(\lambda^{\prime})-n(\nu^{\prime})}\frac{c_{\lambda}c_{\nu}}{c_{\mu}}f^{\mu}_{\lambda,\nu}

and combining this with (
) leads to
(
6.2
).
∎

Next we define the mixed Cauchy kernel
K(x;z)
which we will expand in
F_{\lambda}(x)
and apply to it the evaluation map ev
μ/ν
using the result (
6.2
) of the above Lemma.

Definition 6.2
.

Let
(z)
and
(y)
be two alphabets and
\Pi(z,y)
the Cauchy kernel (
2.15
).
Define the mixed Cauchy kernel:

(6.3)

\displaystyle K(x;y):=\iota_{z}^{-1}\left(\Pi(z,y)\right)

Remark 6.3
.

For
K(x;y)
we have the analogues of the exponential formula (
2.15
) as well as bases expansions (
2.17
) for
\Pi(z,y)
:

(6.4)

\displaystyle K(x;y)

\displaystyle=\exp_{*}\left(\sum_{r>0}\frac{1}{r}\frac{1-t^{r}}{1-q^{r}}\frac{(t-q)^{r}}{(1-q)^{r}}p_{r}(y)S_{r}\right)

(6.5)

\displaystyle K(x;y)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\lambda}\frac{(1-t)^{|\lambda|}}{(1-q)^{|\lambda|}}m_{\lambda}(y)E_{\lambda}(t^{-1})

(6.6)

\displaystyle K(x;y)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\lambda}\frac{q^{n(\lambda^{\prime})}(1-t)^{|\lambda|}}{c^{\prime}_{\lambda}}P_{\lambda}(y)F_{\lambda}

Proof.

The exponential formula (
6.4
) is obtained by inserting the exponential formula (
2.15
) for the Cauchy kernel in (
6.3
) and applying
\iota_{z}
to the power sums (
3.48
). The monomial expansion (
6.5
) is a consequence of (
2.17
) and the isomorphism formula (
3.49
)
7
7

7

The monomial expansion of
K(x;z)
was studied in
[
15
]
in relation with the tableaux formula for the Macdonald functions.
. The Macdonald expansion (
6.6
) follows from (
2.17
), (
2.9
) and the isomorphism formula (
3.45
).
∎

Lemma 6.4
.

Let
\mu/\nu
be a skew partition and
k=|\mu|-|\nu|
. The mixed Cauchy kernel
K(x;y)
reproduces the skew Macdonald function
P_{\mu/\nu}(y)
under
{\text{ev}}_{\mu/\nu}
:

(6.7)

\displaystyle P_{\mu/\nu}(y)=\tilde{a}_{\mu,\nu}{\text{ev}}_{\mu/\nu}\left(K(x;y)\right),\qquad\tilde{a}_{\mu,\nu}:=\frac{q^{n(\nu^{\prime})-n(\mu^{\prime})}c_{\mu}^{\prime}d_{\mu/\nu}}{(1-t)^{k}c_{\nu}^{\prime}}

Proof.

We first note that the evaluation map
\text{ev}_{\mu/\nu}
acts on
F\in\mathcal{A}^{\circ}_{j}
and gives zero unless
j=k
, the number of boxes in
\mu/\nu
. We apply
\text{ev}_{\mu/\nu}
to
K(x;y)
written in the form (
6.6
):

\displaystyle\text{ev}_{\mu/\nu}\left(K(x;y)\right)=

\displaystyle(1-t)^{k}\sum_{\lambda\vdash k}\frac{q^{n(\lambda^{\prime})}}{c^{\prime}_{\lambda}}P_{\lambda}(y)\text{ev}_{\mu/\nu}\left(F_{\lambda}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(1-t)^{k}\frac{q^{n(\mu^{\prime})-n(\nu^{\prime})}c_{\nu}}{c_{\mu}d_{\mu/\nu}}\sum_{\lambda\vdash k}b_{\lambda}f^{\mu}_{\lambda,\nu}P_{\lambda}(y)

\displaystyle=\frac{(1-t)^{k}q^{n(\mu^{\prime})-n(\nu^{\prime})}c_{\nu}^{\prime}}{c_{\mu}^{\prime}d_{\mu/\nu}}P_{\mu/\nu}(y)

where in the second line we computed
\text{ev}_{\mu/\nu}(F_{\lambda})
using (
6.2
) and then used the definition of
b_{\lambda}
(
2.9
). To get the result in the third line we recalled the formula for the skew Macdonald functions (
2.14
):

P_{\mu/\nu}=\sum_{\lambda}\frac{b_{\nu}b_{\lambda}}{b_{\mu}}f^{\mu}_{\lambda,\nu}P_{\lambda}

This computation proves (
6.7
).
∎

6.2.
The trace elements and the skew Macdonald functions

The elements of the shuffle algebra
S_{r}\in\mathcal{A}^{\circ}_{r}
are mapped to the power sums (
3.48
) under
\iota
, therefore the exponential generating function
T(v;z,w)
given in (
5.48
) is related to the Cauchy kernel (
2.15
) under this isomorphism.

The generating function
T(v;z,w)
(
5.48
) is a symmetric function in two finite alphabets
(w)=(w_{1}\ldots w_{m})
and
(z)=(z_{1}\ldots z_{n})
and can be written in terms of the power sums:

(6.8)

\displaystyle T(v;z,w)=\exp_{*}\left(\sum_{r>0}\frac{1}{r}\frac{1-t^{r}}{1-q^{r}}\left(p_{r}(w)-p_{r}(z)-\frac{q^{r}-t^{r}}{1-t^{r}}z_{0}^{r}\right)v^{r}S_{r}\right)

Therefore it is given by the mixed Cauchy kernel
K(x;y)
to which one needs to apply the following transformation:

(6.9)

\displaystyle\pi_{w,z}:\quad p_{r}(y)\rightarrow p_{r}(w_{1}\ldots w_{m})-p_{r}(z_{1}\ldots z_{n})-\frac{q^{r}-t^{r}}{1-t^{r}}z_{0}^{r}

The map
\pi_{w,z}
is a
plethystic substitution
and for a symmetric function
f(y)
we write:

(6.10)

\displaystyle\pi_{w,z}\left(f(y)\right)=f\left[w-z-\frac{q-t}{1-t}z_{0}\right]

By comparing (
6.8
) with (
6.4
) we find:

(6.11)

\displaystyle T(cv;z,w)=\pi_{w,z}\left(K(x;y)\right),\qquad c=\frac{t-q}{1-q}

This identity together with Lemma
6.4
leads to the following result:

Theorem 6.5
.

Let
\mu/\nu
be a skew partition and
N=|\mu|-|\nu|
, we have the identity:

(6.12)

\displaystyle P_{\mu/\nu}\left[w-z-\frac{q-t}{1-t}z_{0}\right]=a_{\mu,\nu}{\text{ev}}_{\mu/\nu}\left(T_{N}(x;z,w)\right),

where

(6.13)

\displaystyle a_{\mu,\nu}:=\frac{(t-q)^{N}}{(1-q)^{N}(1-t)^{N}}\frac{q^{n(\nu^{\prime})-n(\mu^{\prime})}c_{\mu}^{\prime}d_{\mu/\nu}}{c_{\nu}^{\prime}}

6.3.
Example

In this section we show how to compute
P_{(2,1)/(1)}(w_{1},w_{2};z_{0})
using Theorem
6.5
. In this case we have
N=2
,
n=0
and
m=2
, so according to Theorem
6.5
we need to calculate:

a_{(2,1),(1)}^{-1}P_{(2,1)/(1)}(w_{1},w_{2};z_{0})=\text{ev}_{(2,1)/(1)}\left(T_{2}(x_{1},x_{2};z_{0},w_{1},w_{2})\right)=T_{2}(q,t^{-1};z_{0},w_{1},w_{2})

where
T_{2}(q,t^{-1};z_{0},w_{1},w_{2})
is the partition function of lattice path configurations with two fermionic colours and boundary conditions as in (
4.58
). Let the two colours be red and green where red corresponds to the smaller label and the local configurations are given by the Boltzmann weights of the
R
-matrix of
U_{t}(\widehat{sl}_{1|2})
, given in (
5.1
). We can find all possible configurations in
T_{2}(q,t^{-1};z_{0},w_{1},w_{2})
and write:

(6.14)

\displaystyle a_{(2,1),(1)}^{-1}P_{(2,1)/(1)}(w_{1},w_{2};z_{0})=\hbox to66.22pt{\vbox to67.09pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 14.80403pt\lower-0.2pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}

By substituting the Boltzmann weights (
5.1
) we compute the coefficients in front of each monomial:

(6.15)

\displaystyle P_{(2,1)/(1)}(w_{1},w_{2};z_{0})=

\displaystyle a_{(2,1),(1)}\Big(z^{2}_{0}+\frac{(1-t)(2+q+t+2qt)}{(1+q)(1+t)(t-q)}(z_{0}w_{1}+z_{0}w_{2})

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\frac{(1-t)\left(1-qt^{2}\right)}{(1+q)(1+t)(q-t)^{2}}(w^{2}_{1}+w^{2}_{2})+\frac{(1-t)^{2}(2+q+t+2qt)}{(1+q)(1+t)(q-t)^{2}}w_{1}w_{2}\Big)

where the coefficient
a_{(2,1),(1)}
reads:

(6.16)

\displaystyle a_{(2,1),(1)}=\frac{(1+q)(1+t)(q-t)^{2}}{(1-t)(1-qt^{2})}

Therefore we computed
P_{(2,1)/(1)}(w_{1},w_{2};z_{0})
. By setting
z_{0}=0
in (
6.15
) we recover the standard skew Macdonald polynomial
P_{(2,1)/(1)}(w_{1},w_{2})
:

P_{(2,1)/(1)}(w_{1},w_{2})=w_{1}^{2}+\frac{(1-t)(2+q+t+2qt)}{1-qt^{2}}w_{1}w_{2}+w_{2}^{2}

Appendix A
A shuffle product identity

The determinant formula (
3.13
) for
H_{k}(t^{-1})
is known as the Izergin determinant. This determinant satisfies various summation identities. For our purposes a useful identity is given by a subset formula from
[
5
]
:

\displaystyle\frac{\prod_{i,j=1}^{k}(x_{i}-y_{j})}{\prod_{1\leq i<j\leq k}(x_{i}-x_{j})(y_{j}-y_{i})}\det_{1\leq i,j\leq k}\frac{(1-t)x_{i}}{(x_{i}-y_{j})(y_{j}-tx_{i})}

(A.1)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{r=0}^{k}(-1)^{r}t^{r(r-1)/2}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}S\subseteq[k]\\
|S|=r\end{subarray}}\prod_{i\in S}\prod_{j\in S^{c}}\frac{x_{j}-tx_{i}}{x_{j}-x_{i}}\prod_{i\in S}\prod_{j=1}^{k}\frac{y_{j}-x_{i}}{y_{j}-tx_{i}}

When we set
y_{i}=qx_{i}
the left-hand side above matches with the formula (
3.13
) for
H_{k}(t^{-1})
up to a factor. This leads to a summation formula for
H_{k}(t^{-1})
:

(A.2)

\displaystyle H_{k}(t^{-1})=\frac{(1-qt^{-1})^{k}}{(1-t^{-1})^{k}}\sum_{r=0}^{k}(-1)^{r}t^{-r(r+1)/2}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}S\subseteq[k]\\
|S|=r\end{subarray}}\prod_{i\in S}\prod_{j\in S^{c}}\frac{x_{i}-t^{-1}x_{j}}{x_{i}-x_{j}}\prod_{i\in S}\prod_{j=1}^{k}\frac{x_{i}-qx_{j}}{x_{i}-qt^{-1}x_{j}}

The sum over subsets
S
has the structure of the shuffle product (
3.8
). To see this we split the product over
j
in the last factor in (
A.2
)
into two products:
j\in S
and
j\in S^{c}
. The second product can be combined with the first factor in (
A.2
) giving:

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}S\subseteq[k]\\
|S|=r\end{subarray}}\prod_{i\in S}\prod_{j\in S^{c}}\frac{x_{i}-t^{-1}x_{j}}{x_{i}-x_{j}}\prod_{i\in S}\prod_{j=1}^{k}\frac{x_{i}-qx_{j}}{x_{i}-qt^{-1}x_{j}}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}S\subseteq[k]\\
|S|=r\end{subarray}}\prod_{i\in S}\prod_{j\in S^{c}}\frac{x_{i}-t^{-1}x_{j}}{x_{i}-x_{j}}\frac{x_{i}-qx_{j}}{x_{i}-qt^{-1}x_{j}}\prod_{i,j\in S}\frac{x_{i}-qx_{j}}{x_{i}-qt^{-1}x_{j}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{(1-q)^{r}}{(1-qt^{-1})^{r}}t^{r(r-1)/2}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}S\subseteq[k]\\
|S|=r\end{subarray}}E_{r}(q;x_{S})\prod_{i\in S}\prod_{j\in S^{c}}\zeta(x_{j}/x_{i})=\frac{(1-q)^{r}}{(1-qt^{-1})^{r}}t^{r(r-1)/2}E_{k-r}(tq^{-1})*E_{r}(q)

where in the second line we used (
3.2
), Definition
3.4
of
E_{r}(q)
and
E_{k-r}(tq^{-1})
(which is equal to
1
) and the formula for the shuffle product (
3.8
). As a consequence the element
H_{k}(t^{-1})
admits the formula:

(A.3)

\displaystyle H_{k}(t^{-1})=\frac{(1-qt^{-1})^{k}}{(1-t^{-1})^{k}}\sum_{r=0}^{k}(-t)^{-r}\frac{(1-q)^{r}}{(1-qt^{-1})^{r}}E_{k-r}(tq^{-1})*E_{r}(q)

We can derive analogously the identities for
H_{k}(q)
and
H_{k}(tq^{-1})
and summarize all three formulas by:

(A.4)

\displaystyle H_{k}(q_{a})=\sum_{r=0}^{k}q_{c}^{k-r}\left(\frac{1-q_{b}}{1-q_{b}q_{c}}\right)^{k-r}\left(\frac{1-q_{c}}{1-q_{b}q_{c}}\right)^{r}E_{k-r}(q_{b})*E_{r}(q_{c})

where
(a,b,c)
is a permutation of
(1,2,3)
.

Appendix B
The supersymmetric
F
-matrix

In this section we discuss the connection between our
F
-matrix (
5.14
) and the graded
F
-matrix of
[
44
]
and prove Lemma
5.4
.

B.1.
The supersymmetric
F
-matrix and gradation

Let
R^{\star}
and
F^{\star}
be the
R
and
F
matrices of
[
44
]
of the algebra
U_{t}(\widehat{sl}_{n+1|m})
. We rewrite
R^{\star}
and
F^{\star}
in the
multiplicative
convention (set
x=e^{-2u}
and
t=e^{-2\eta}
to recover the
additive
convention of
[
44
]
) and label vectors from
0
to
n+m
(as opposed to
1
to
n+m+1
as in
[
44
]
):

(B.1)

\displaystyle R^{\star}(x)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=0}^{n+m}\left(\frac{t-x}{1-tx}\right)^{\delta_{i<m}}E^{(ii)}\otimes E^{(ii)}+t^{1/2}\frac{1-x}{1-tx}\sum_{0\leq i\neq j\leq n+m}E^{(ii)}\otimes E^{(jj)}+

\displaystyle+\frac{1-t}{1-tx}\sum_{0\leq j\neq i\leq n+m}(-1)^{\delta_{i<m}\delta_{j<m}}x^{\delta_{i>j}}E^{(ij)}\otimes E^{(ji)}

and:

(B.2)

\displaystyle F^{*}_{N}(x_{1}\dots x_{N})=\sum_{\sigma\in\mathcal{S}_{N}}\sum_{(k_{1}\dots k_{N})\in\mathcal{J}_{\sigma}}\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq i<j\leq N\\
k_{i}=k_{j}<m\end{subarray}}(1+t)\frac{x_{\sigma(i)}-x_{\sigma(j)}}{x_{\sigma(i)}-tx_{\sigma(j)}}\cdot\prod_{i=1}^{N}E^{(k_{i}k_{i})}_{\sigma(i)}R^{*}_{\sigma}

The transformation between
R(x)
from (
5.8
) and
R^{\star}(x)
involves: change of
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
gradation of the vector space, spectral parameter dependent gauge transform, relabeling of the basis vectors
8
8

8

The fermionic labels in (
B.1
) are associated with the vectors
\left|0\right\rangle,\ldots,\left|m-1\right\rangle
and in our conventions the fermionic labels are associated with the vectors
\left|n+1\right\rangle,\ldots,\left|n+m\right\rangle
.
and a transformation known as the stochastic twist (see e.g.
[
30
]
). In this section we focus on the change of
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
gradation because this transformation affects the rational function appearing in (
B.2
) and explains the form of the
F
-matrix in (
5.14
).

The change of
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
gradation can be expressed using two diagonal matrices:

(B.3)

\displaystyle D_{l}:=\sum_{i,j=0}^{n+m}(-1)^{\delta_{j<i<m}}E^{(ii)}\otimes E^{(jj)},\qquad D_{r}:=\sum_{i,j=0}^{n+m}(-1)^{\delta_{j\leq i<m}}E^{(ii)}\otimes E^{(jj)}

We define the
R
-matrix
\tilde{R}
:

(B.4)

\displaystyle\tilde{R}(x):=D_{l}R^{\star}(x)D_{r}=D_{r}R^{\star}(x)D_{l}

With the present definitions we have:

(B.5)

\displaystyle R^{\star}(1)=P^{\star},\qquad\tilde{R}(1)=P,

(B.6)

\displaystyle P=D_{l}P^{\star}D_{r}=D_{r}P^{\star}D_{l}

where
P^{\star}
is the graded permutation matrix. The
R^{\star}
matrix satisfies the graded Yang–Baxter equation (see
[
44
]
) while
\tilde{R}
satisfies the standard Yang–Baxter equation. The
R^{\star}
and
F^{\star}
matrices satisfy:

(B.7)

\displaystyle F^{*}_{N}(x)=P^{*}_{\sigma}F^{*}_{N}(x_{\sigma})P_{\sigma^{-1}}^{*}R^{*}_{\sigma}

and
\tilde{R}
satisfies:

(B.8)

\displaystyle\tilde{F}_{N}(x)=P_{\sigma}\tilde{F}_{N}(x_{\sigma})P_{\sigma^{-1}}\tilde{R}_{\sigma}

where the matrix
\tilde{F}
can be chosen to be of the form (
5.14
) (up to a relabeling of the basis vectors):

(B.9)

\displaystyle\tilde{F}_{N}(x_{1}\dots x_{N})=\sum_{\sigma\in\mathcal{S}_{N}}\sum_{(k_{1}\dots k_{N})\in\mathcal{J}_{\sigma}}\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq i<j\leq N\\
k_{i}=k_{j}\\
k_{i}<m\end{subarray}}\frac{x_{\sigma(i)}+x_{\sigma(j)}}{x_{\sigma(i)}-tx_{\sigma(j)}}\cdot\prod_{i=1}^{N}E^{(k_{i}k_{i})}_{\sigma(i)}\tilde{R}_{\sigma}

The essential difference between
F^{\star}
and
\tilde{F}
is that in
\tilde{F}
the factors
x_{\sigma(i)}-x_{\sigma(j)}
are absent.
The matrices
\tilde{F}
and
F^{\star}
are related by:

(B.10)

\displaystyle\tilde{F}_{N}(x)=X_{N}(x)D_{N}F^{\star}_{N}(x)D_{N}

where:

(B.11)

\displaystyle X_{N}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\alpha\in\{0\ldots n+m\}^{N}}\prod_{1\leq i<j\leq N}\left(\frac{1}{1+t}\frac{x_{i}+x_{j}}{x_{i}-x_{j}}\right)^{\delta_{\alpha_{j}=\alpha_{i}<m}}\bigotimes_{i=1}^{N}E^{(\alpha_{i}\alpha_{i})}

(B.12)

\displaystyle D_{N}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\alpha\in\{0\ldots n+m\}^{N}}\prod_{1\leq i<j\leq N}(-1)^{\delta_{\alpha_{j}\leq\alpha_{i}<m}}\bigotimes_{i=1}^{N}E^{(\alpha_{i}\alpha_{i})}

Checking (
B.10
) amounts to performing elementary algebraic manipulations. By using (
B.10
) and (
B.7
) one can prove (
B.8
):

\displaystyle\tilde{F}_{N}(x)=X_{N}(x)D_{N}F^{\star}_{N}(x)D_{N}

\displaystyle=X_{N}(x)D_{N}P^{\star}_{\sigma}F^{\star}_{N}(x_{\sigma})P^{\star}_{\sigma^{-1}}R^{\star}_{\sigma}D_{N}

\displaystyle=P_{\sigma}X_{N}(x_{\sigma})D_{N}F^{\star}_{N}(x_{\sigma})P^{\star}_{\sigma^{-1}}R^{\star}_{\sigma}D_{N}

\displaystyle=P_{\sigma}X_{N}(x_{\sigma})D_{N}F^{\star}_{N}(x_{\sigma})D_{N}P_{\sigma^{-1}}R_{\sigma}=P_{\sigma}\tilde{F}_{N}(x_{\sigma})P_{\sigma^{-1}}R_{\sigma}

where in the first line we used (
B.7
), in the second and third lines we used the following equations:

(B.13)

\displaystyle X_{N}(x)D_{N}P^{\star}_{\sigma}=P_{\sigma}X_{N}(x_{\sigma})D_{N},\qquad P^{\star}_{\sigma^{-1}}R^{\star}_{\sigma}D_{N}=D_{N}P_{\sigma^{-1}}R_{\sigma}

and the final equality is due to (
B.10
). In the first equation in (
B.13
) the antisymmetry of the factors
x_{i}-x_{j}
in
X_{N}
plays an important role.

B.2.
Proof of Lemma
5.4

The
F
-matrix
F_{N}
, given in (
5.14
), contains sectors labelled by compositions of non-negative integers
(l_{0},l_{1}\ldots l_{n+m})
, such that
N=l_{0}+\cdots+l_{n+m}
. Moreover, it is also lower-triangular. Due to our labeling of the basis vectors these two aspects imply that the vectors
\left|\lambda^{+}\right\rangle
and covectors
\left\langle\lambda^{-}\right|
, with:

(B.14)

\displaystyle\lambda^{-}:=(0^{l_{0}}1^{l_{1}}\ldots(n+m)^{l_{n+m}}),\qquad\lambda^{+}:=((n+m)^{l_{n+m}}\ldots 1^{l_{1}}0^{l_{0}})

must be right end left eigenvectors of
F_{N}
respectively. Therefore in order to prove (
5.18
) we need to compute the matrix elements:

(B.15)

\displaystyle\left\langle\lambda^{\pm}\right|F_{N}(x)\left|\lambda^{\pm}\right\rangle

From (
5.14
) it follows that we can write all matrix elements of the
F
-matrix in terms of matrix elements of
R_{\sigma}
:

(B.16)

\displaystyle\left\langle\lambda\right|F_{N}(x)\left|\mu\right\rangle=\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq i<j\leq N\\
\lambda_{\sigma(i)}=\lambda_{\sigma(j)}>n\end{subarray}}\frac{x_{\sigma(i)}+x_{\sigma(j)}}{x_{\sigma(i)}-tx_{\sigma(j)}}\cdot\left\langle\lambda\right|R_{\sigma}(x)\left|\mu\right\rangle

where
\sigma
is any permutation such that:

(B.17)

\displaystyle\lambda_{\sigma(1)}\leq\cdots\leq\lambda_{\sigma(N)}

By choosing
\lambda=\mu=\lambda^{-}
in (
B.16
) and
\sigma
to be the identity permutation we get:

(B.18)

\displaystyle\left\langle\lambda^{-}\right|F_{N}(x)\left|\lambda^{-}\right\rangle=\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq i<j\leq N\\
\lambda_{i}^{-}=\lambda_{j}^{-}>n\end{subarray}}\frac{x_{i}+x_{j}}{x_{i}-tx_{j}}

Next we set
\lambda=\mu=\lambda^{+}
in (
B.16
). The permutation
\sigma
must be chosen such that:

\sigma(\lambda^{+})=\lambda^{-}

We select
\sigma
in a way that does not involve permutations of labels of
\lambda^{+}
which are the same. This means that
\sigma
needs to consist of factors of the form
\sigma_{(j,k)}
which perform local cyclic permutations:

\sigma_{(j,k)}(\ldots j^{l_{j}}k^{l_{k}}\ldots)=(\ldots k^{l_{k}}j^{l_{j}}\ldots),\qquad j>k

leaving the labels in
\ldots
untouched. We use
R_{\sigma}=P_{\sigma}\check{R}_{\sigma}
(see (
4.36
)) and compute:

(B.19)

\displaystyle\left\langle\lambda^{+}\right|F_{N}(x)\left|\lambda^{+}\right\rangle=\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq i<j\leq N\\
\lambda_{\sigma(i)}^{+}=\lambda_{\sigma(j)}^{+}>n\end{subarray}}\frac{x_{\sigma(i)}+x_{\sigma(j)}}{x_{\sigma(i)}-tx_{\sigma(j)}}\cdot\left\langle\lambda^{+}\right|P_{\sigma}\check{R}_{\sigma}\left|\lambda^{+}\right\rangle=\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq i<j\leq N\\
\lambda_{i}^{+}=\lambda_{j}^{+}>n\end{subarray}}\frac{x_{i}+x_{j}}{x_{i}-tx_{j}}\cdot\left\langle\lambda^{-}\right|\check{R}_{\sigma}\left|\lambda^{+}\right\rangle

For a demonstration of how
\left\langle\lambda^{-}\right|\check{R}_{\sigma}\left|\lambda^{+}\right\rangle
is computed we consider the case
n+m=2
. Using the graphical notation for
\check{R}_{i}
from (
4.16
) we have:

(B.20)

\displaystyle\left\langle\lambda^{-}\right|\check{R}_{\sigma}\left|\lambda^{+}\right\rangle=\hbox to124.47pt{\vbox to159.33pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 5.32607pt\lower-68.28285pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}

The only possible local configurations in the above diagram correspond to the fourth vertex in the first row and third vertex in the second row in (
5.1
) such that the labels at the top follow the lines. By computing the Boltzmann weights we arrive at:

\left\langle\lambda^{-}\right|\check{R}_{\sigma}\left|\lambda^{+}\right\rangle=\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq i<j\leq N\\
\lambda^{+}_{i}\neq\lambda^{+}_{j}\end{subarray}}t^{\delta_{\lambda^{+}_{j}>0}}\frac{x_{i}-x_{j}}{x_{i}-tx_{j}}

Combining this with (
B.19
) gives us the eigenvalue:

(B.21)

\displaystyle\left\langle\lambda^{+}\right|F_{N}(x)\left|\lambda^{+}\right\rangle=\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq i<j\leq N\\
\lambda_{i}^{+}=\lambda_{j}^{+}>n\end{subarray}}\frac{x_{i}+x_{j}}{x_{i}-tx_{j}}\cdot\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq i<j\leq N\\
\lambda^{+}_{i}\neq\lambda^{+}_{j}\end{subarray}}t^{\delta_{\lambda^{+}_{j}>0}}\frac{x_{i}-x_{j}}{x_{i}-tx_{j}}

The expressions (
B.18
) and (
B.21
) are precisely the eigenvalues appearing in (
5.18
) and the Lemma follows.
